questions just like how how was it

to this week and not just like

like boom boom boom like

yeah but also there's a moment because

i've interviewed a bunch of people for

like teams and stuff yeah there's also a

moment when you early on no it's not

00:44:41 --> 00:44:47
gonna be a good fit yeah and you still

have to land that plane and yeah and all

00:44:46 --> 00:44:50
that kind of stuff and that could get

00:44:47 --> 00:44:52
really really really exhausting so yeah

00:44:49 --> 00:44:54
anyway sorry so

00:44:51 --> 00:44:55
yeah so basically we tried all we've

00:44:53 --> 00:44:56
tried so many things over the years to

00:44:54 --> 00:44:59
make interviews more efficient because

it's a huge time sync for um the team

00:44:58 --> 00:45:01
so

00:44:59 --> 00:45:04
you know we we basically will usually

00:45:00 --> 00:45:05
get them down to like 25 minutes

00:45:03 --> 00:45:07
i've seen if you're trying to hire like

a big team let's say

00:45:06 --> 00:45:10
um

00:45:07 --> 00:45:11
you know of people who are like

contractors or something not necessarily

00:45:10 --> 00:45:15
full-time employees i've seen people

00:45:11 --> 00:45:16
actually do 10-minute interviews

00:45:14 --> 00:45:18
you can even interview like a thousand

00:45:15 --> 00:45:19
people almost like in a week or

00:45:17 --> 00:45:21
something i'm not sure if that quite

00:45:18 --> 00:45:23
works out but let me a little less than

00:45:20 --> 00:45:25
that but you can basically get six and

00:45:22 --> 00:45:26
six done in an hour if you're just i

00:45:24 --> 00:45:27
need to get a team of 30 contractors for

00:45:25 --> 00:45:29
whatever purpose but if you're talking

00:45:26 --> 00:45:31

00:45:28 --> 00:45:33
like 25 minute

00:45:30 --> 00:45:34
you know you're oftentimes like

one one thing we've done is we'll put

00:45:34 --> 00:45:36
like a

00:45:35 --> 00:45:39
um

00:45:35 --> 00:45:40
like a google form online and it's like

00:45:38 --> 00:45:41
put some basic hurdles in there like you

know ask them

00:45:40 --> 00:45:44
um

00:45:42 --> 00:45:45
to put in an answer which you can check

in a spreadsheet if it was correct or

not and like there were some funny

examples in the early days of coinbase

where we put in like brain teasers and

00:45:48 --> 00:45:53
stuff but we don't do that anymore we do

00:45:50 --> 00:45:55
like normal interviews we do references

00:45:52 --> 00:45:56
the kinds of things i ask in interviews

00:45:54 --> 00:45:58
um

00:45:56 --> 00:46:00
you know it's usually like

00:45:57 --> 00:46:02
i like to think about what what do we

00:45:59 --> 00:46:03
need this person to accomplish in this

00:46:01 --> 00:46:04
role right

00:46:02 --> 00:46:06
and

00:46:04 --> 00:46:08
get really specific about that it's like

usually something pretty hard and then

i'll ask them a question it's like tell

00:46:09 --> 00:46:13
me about a time you did x

00:46:11 --> 00:46:15
and um

00:46:12 --> 00:46:17
or tell me about the hardest the hardest

kind of problem you've had to solve in

why

00:46:17 --> 00:46:21
and what did you do specifically to

00:46:18 --> 00:46:22
vercome it right

00:46:20 --> 00:46:23
so i'm asking to see if they can

00:46:21 --> 00:46:25
actually do the stuff we need to get

00:46:22 --> 00:46:26
done but then i'm also kind of asking

00:46:24 --> 00:46:28
inter like culture questions if i'm

00:46:25 --> 00:46:30
interviewing for that

00:46:27 --> 00:46:32
and so i'm trying to see like are they

concise communicators can they just give

00:46:32 --> 00:46:36
answer

and stop talking some people like ramble

on for like five ten minutes if you ask

00:46:37 --> 00:46:42
the first question some people are you

00:46:39 --> 00:46:44
know their interrupter like church of

00:46:41 --> 00:46:45
interruption so like they won't stop

00:46:43 --> 00:46:47
talking until you interrupt them which

00:46:44 --> 00:46:50
for me i know i'm always patient and i

00:46:46 --> 00:46:52
wait so yeah that's weird

00:46:49 --> 00:46:54
i'm looking to see for humility too like

00:46:51 --> 00:46:56
you know i'll tell ask people well tell

00:46:53 --> 00:46:57
me about a time something went really

wrong like you had conflict or someone

on a team or

were they part of the solution or are

they still holding on to like blame and

00:47:03 --> 00:47:07
criticism about that and be like well i

told them it shouldn't do that way but

00:47:06 --> 00:47:10
hey didn't listen to me you know these

00:47:07 --> 00:47:12
are all like bad signs so i'm looking

00:47:10 --> 00:47:14
for yeah can they get the job done will

00:47:11 --> 00:47:16
they work together on a team

00:47:13 --> 00:47:18
can they communicate effectively

00:47:15 --> 00:47:19
do they fit into our cultural values and

00:47:17 --> 00:47:22
you know those kind of things yeah i

00:47:18 --> 00:47:24
mean there's uh because i've

00:47:21 --> 00:47:26
uh even even for help with this this

00:47:23 --> 00:47:29
podcast here but also at mit and so on

00:47:25 --> 00:47:30
i've done a bunch of hiring and uh i was

00:47:28 --> 00:47:32
always looking for you said brain

00:47:29 --> 00:47:33
teasers all kinds of simple questions

00:47:31 --> 00:47:36
that can

00:47:32 --> 00:47:38

00:47:37 --> 00:47:41
to i still asked this question but

00:47:40 --> 00:47:43
uh

00:47:40 --> 00:47:46
do you think it's better to work hard or

00:47:42 --> 00:47:47
work smart

00:47:45 --> 00:47:50
and

00:47:46 --> 00:47:51
you know i had this idea

00:47:49 --> 00:47:55
that

00:47:50 --> 00:47:58
i've that i think i've matured about

00:47:54 --> 00:48:02
which is i kind of believe that people

00:47:57 --> 00:48:05
who say work smart on that question

00:48:01 --> 00:48:07
don't actually work smart so the right

00:48:04 --> 00:48:08
extbook answers what it's better to

00:48:06 --> 00:48:11
work smart

00:48:07 --> 00:48:13
but the reality is it's

00:48:10 --> 00:48:14
people that haven't actually ever

00:48:12 --> 00:48:16
done anything that

00:48:13 --> 00:48:19
say work smart they're like

00:48:15 --> 00:48:21
they haven't really struggled because my

00:48:18 --> 00:48:23
general belief at the time

00:48:20 --> 00:48:25
was in order to discover

00:48:22 --> 00:48:27
what it means to

00:48:24 --> 00:48:30
work smart to be efficient to you have

00:48:26 --> 00:48:33
to work your ass off so you have to

00:48:29 --> 00:48:34
really fail a lot and failure feels like

00:48:32 --> 00:48:37
hard work

00:48:33 --> 00:48:39
and so i was always suspicious of people

00:48:36 --> 00:48:41
that would say work smart i would want

00:48:38 --> 00:48:44
o interrogate that question

00:48:40 --> 00:48:46
um but then i also you know have have

00:48:43 --> 00:48:50
learned that there is

00:48:45 --> 00:48:52
people that are just exceptionally um

00:48:49 --> 00:48:54
exceptionally efficient

00:48:51 --> 00:48:56
hey really do know what it means to

00:48:53 --> 00:48:58
work smart even at a young age

00:48:55 --> 00:49:01
and so like you can't just disqualify

00:48:57 --> 00:49:02
based on that you have to dig in deeper

00:49:00 --> 00:49:04
but some of the most interesting people

00:49:02 --> 00:49:06
i've ever worked with would say work

00:49:03 --> 00:49:08
hard unapologetically and they're

00:49:05 --> 00:49:10
usually the ones that know how to be

00:49:07 --> 00:49:12
fficient which is it's just an

00:49:09 --> 00:49:14
interesting thing like that and and i've

00:49:11 --> 00:49:16
always searched for questions of that

00:49:13 --> 00:49:19
nature to see

00:49:15 --> 00:49:21
can i can i get

00:49:18 --> 00:49:23
a person to reveal something

00:49:20 --> 00:49:27
profound about them

00:49:22 --> 00:49:29
in as brief of a question as possible

00:49:26 --> 00:49:31
and i you know and then of course

00:49:28 --> 00:49:33
there's basic attention to detail and

00:49:30 --> 00:49:35
brain teasers and stuff like that

00:49:32 --> 00:49:37
depending on the role programming and so

00:49:34 --> 00:49:38
n to see can they can they solve a

00:49:36 --> 00:49:41
tricky puzzle

00:49:37 --> 00:49:44
and do so like one that doesn't require

00:49:40 --> 00:49:47
a lot of effort but requires a certain

00:49:43 --> 00:49:48
um non-linear way of thinking is there

00:49:46 --> 00:49:50
some

00:49:48 --> 00:49:52
i mean maybe you don't want to reveal

00:49:49 --> 00:49:54
but is there some questions

00:49:51 --> 00:49:55
that you sometimes find yourself leaning

00:49:53 --> 00:49:58
on

00:49:54 --> 00:50:00
you said like how did you solve

00:49:57 --> 00:50:02
a hard problem in your past

00:49:59 --> 00:50:04
and have them talk through it that's one

you know we started with brain teasers

00:50:04 --> 00:50:08
periodically at coinbase and i we got we

00:50:06 --> 00:50:09
got away from that relatively quickly

00:50:07 --> 00:50:12
and i think

00:50:08 --> 00:50:13
one i it's a tough one because i

actually think

00:50:12 --> 00:50:17
it does show how somebody

kind of performs under pressure but it's

00:50:16 --> 00:50:20
i don't think it's a super reliable

who are really

good

in what the typical work situation but

00:50:25 --> 00:50:28
hat's not a typical work situation

00:50:26 --> 00:50:30
where somebody puts you on the spot like

in a live interview and sometimes people

and like they don't have their computer

in front of them or whatever they

00:50:33 --> 00:50:38
normally use

so

00:50:37 --> 00:50:43
yeah i i'm a little skeptical now of the

00:50:42 --> 00:50:45
there is a whole

yeah

there is a whole question about like a

o get people in the door because the

used to which is the whole topic but

yeah that's fascinating because um

uh from the the ruby wallet days

um

00:52:02 --> 00:52:08

so post product market fit yeah a lot of

00:52:09 --> 00:52:14
it's scaling and you got to build up

00:52:11 --> 00:52:15
build out an actual company so

00:52:13 --> 00:52:17
i remember i was still writing a lot of

00:52:14 --> 00:52:19
code there for a while and we were we

00:52:16 --> 00:52:20
re hiring in we had like maybe 25

00:52:18 --> 00:52:22
people or something i remember one of

our investors came by one day and he was

00:52:21 --> 00:52:26
like brian how much your time are you

00:52:22 --> 00:52:28

00:52:25 --> 00:52:30
maybe maybe 50 or something he was like

how much time are you spending hiring

00:52:29 --> 00:52:34
people i was like probably 20

00:52:31 --> 00:52:36
and he was like

00:52:33 --> 00:52:37
i think you need to flip those numbers

00:52:35 --> 00:52:38
like this company's not gonna scale

00:52:36 --> 00:52:40
you're the ceo you don't need to be

00:52:37 --> 00:52:41
writing code every day

00:52:39 --> 00:52:43
ou're gonna have to transition that

stuff like even if people can't all that

00:52:42 --> 00:52:45
stuff's locked in your head so maybe

00:52:43 --> 00:52:47
they're not gonna do it as well as you

00:52:44 --> 00:52:48
for the first six months or something

00:52:46 --> 00:52:49
but like if you don't start to

00:52:47 --> 00:52:51
transition it you're never gonna build a

00:52:48 --> 00:52:53
real company it's just gonna be

00:52:50 --> 00:52:55
you're gonna be the bottleneck

00:52:52 --> 00:52:57
so you know like a lot of founders

00:52:54 --> 00:52:58
that took me a while to like really

00:52:56 --> 00:53:00
internalize that lesson i'd always heard

00:52:57 --> 00:53:02
people say that and you know

00:52:59 --> 00:53:04
but i still was holding on too much to

00:53:02 --> 00:53:05
decision making and i probably still am

00:53:03 --> 00:53:07
by the way like even to this day at

coinbase where

00:53:06 --> 00:53:10
we uh continually have to push down

decision making in the org like even

with 5000 people like who are the owners

00:53:11 --> 00:53:15
of each of these things and so the

00:53:12 --> 00:53:17
temptation is people to push it up and

you become a bottleneck and anyway

00:53:16 --> 00:53:20
um so yeah you want you basically need

00:53:18 --> 00:53:22
to make sure you have enough money where

you don't die

00:53:21 --> 00:53:25
if there's in some some kind of a

downturn or hit break even profitability

we were in a position where we were

periodically profitable during up

eriods but then crypto would go down

to kind of manage our own psychology and

crypto companies did

um we had to basically professionalize a

whole bunch of services that had been

just very quickly thrown together by

like 20 year olds right

whether that was cyber security it's

but not someone who's so senior that

hey can't get their hands dirty and

they can come into a company with 25 50

people how do you get a finance person

to come and do that our finances were

a mess like we didn't even really know

how much money we had at certain times

and stuff i mean this was it's

i remember

00:54:06 --> 00:54:11
there was a point where um

our series c or something like that and

i think we had our bank accounts i just

have some padding until yes like all

right i can't at least count on this to

right i mean it was like a cheap hack
